### Bear claw

A bear claw is a sweet pastry originating in the United States during the mid-1910s.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Since: 1920-

Fun facts:

- (history) The name bear claw as used for a pastry is first attested in a 1914 newspaper ad for the Geibel German Bakery in Sacramento, California.
- (origin) Bear claw comes from United States.
- (history) France also has an alternate version of that pastry: patte d'ours (meaning bear paw), created in 1982 in the Alps.

### Beef Manhattan

Beef Manhattan is a Midwestern American dish consisting of roast beef and gravy.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): beef, bread, mashed potato, gravy, roast beef

Fun facts:

- (origin) Beef Manhattan was first served in a restaurant under the name "Beef Manhattan" in a now-defunct Indianapolis deli in the late 1940s where it gained traction as a Hoosier staple.
- (history) Beef Manhattan was named by Naval Ordnance Plant, Indianapolis (NOPI) workers who were trained on a fabrication of the Norden bombsight in Manhattan during World War II.
- (origin) Beef Manhattan comes from United States.

### Buffalo wing

A Buffalo wing in American cuisine is an unbreaded chicken wing section (flat or drumette) that is generally deep-fried, then coated or dipped in a sauce consisting of a vinegar-based cayenne pepper hot sauce and melted butter.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Named after: Buffalo
- Ingredients (Wikidata): butter, chicken wing

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) Buffalo wings are named after Buffalo, New York, where they were invented, and have no relation to the animal.
- (culture) They are traditionally served hot with celery and carrot sticks and a dip of ranch dressing or blue cheese dressing.
- (about) Buffalo wings have gained in popularity in the United States and abroad, with some North American restaurant chains featuring them as a main menu item.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (inspiration) Buffalo wing is named after Buffalo.
- (origin) Buffalo wing comes from United States.

California-style pizza (also known as California pizza) is a style of pizza that combines New York and Italian thin crust with toppings from the California cuisine cooking style.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Named after: California

Fun facts:

- (about) Its invention is generally attributed to chef Ed LaDou, and Chez Panisse, in Berkeley, California.
- (inspiration) California-style pizza is named after California.
- (origin) California-style pizza comes from United States.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) Wolfgang Puck, after meeting LaDou, popularized the style of pizza in the rest of the country.

### Lobster Newberg

Lobster Newberg (also spelled lobster Newburg or lobster Newburgh) is an American seafood dish made from lobster, butter, cream, cognac, sherry and eggs, with a secret ingredient found to be Cayenne pepper.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): lobster

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) A modern legend with no primary or early sources states that the dish was invented by Ben Wenberg, a sea captain in the fruit trade.
- (history) He was said to have demonstrated the dish at Delmonico's Restaurant in New York City to the manager, Charles Delmonico, in 1876.
- (about) After refinements by the chef, Charles Ranhofer, the creation was added to the restaurant's menu as Lobster à la Wenberg and it soon became very popular.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (inspiration) The legend says that an argument between Wenberg and Charles Delmonico caused the dish to be removed from the menu, but patrons continued to request it, so the name was rendered in anagram as Lobster à la Newberg or Lobster Newberg.
- (inspiration) However, according to culinary historian Jan Whitaker lobster Newberg is merely the addition of lobster to a French-inspired sauce of a type already common in 1870s American cuisine.
- (origin) Lobster Newberg comes from United States.

### Sloppy joe (New Jersey)

Throughout much of northern New Jersey, a sloppy joe is a cold delicatessen sandwich traditionally made as a double-decker on thin-sliced rye bread.

- From: United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): cold cut, coleslaw, rye bread, Swiss cheese, Russian dressing

Fun facts:

- (history) The Town Hall Deli in South Orange claims to have invented the New Jersey sloppy joe during the 1930s.
- (culture) According to local tradition, Maplewood politician Thomas Sweeney returned from a trip to Cuba after visiting Sloppy Joe's in Havana, where patrons assembled sandwiches from assorted meats and toppings.
- (about) Sweeney later requested similar sandwiches for poker gatherings catered by Town Hall Deli, helping popularize the dish in northern New Jersey.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) During the mid-20th century, the sandwich became popular in Jewish delicatessens throughout Newark and surrounding communities, contributing to its association with the delicatessen culture of the region.
- (about) A related sandwich sometimes known as the New York deli turkey sandwich is also found in the New York metropolitan area.
- (origin) Sloppy joe comes from United States.

### Eggs Sardou

Eggs Sardou is a Louisiana Creole cuisine dish made with poached eggs, artichoke bottoms, creamed spinach and Hollandaise sauce.

- From: Louisiana, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): Louisiana
- Named after: Victorien Sardou
- Ingredients (Wikidata): hollandaise sauce, egg as food, Engraulis, Tuber, globe artichoke, spinach, poached egg

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) Eggs Sardou is named for Victorien Sardou, a famous French dramatist of the 19th century, who was a guest in New Orleans when the dish was invented around the turn of the century (from 19th to 20th).
- (about) Eggs Sardou is on the menu of many Creole restaurants in New Orleans, including Antoine's, where eggs Sardou was invented, and at Brennan's.
- (origin) Eggs Sardou comes from Louisiana.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (inspiration) Eggs Sardou is named after Victorien Sardou.
- (about) Eggs Sardou is related to the famous eggs Benedict, and is considered one of the many variations of that recipe in most cases omitting Canadian bacon and English muffin, and adding artichoke hearts and creamed spinach.

### Red beans and rice

Red beans and rice is an emblematic dish of Louisiana Creole cuisine (not originally of Cajun cuisine) traditionally made on Mondays with small red beans, vegetables (bell pepper, onion, and celery), spices (thyme, cayenne pepper, and bay leaf), and pork bones as left over from Sunday dinner, cooked together slowly in a pot and served over rice.

- Origin (Wikidata): United States

- (culture) Red beans and rice is customary – ham was traditionally a Sunday meal and Monday was washday.

### Shrimp creole

Shrimp creole is a dish of Louisiana Creole origin (French, Spanish, and African heritage), consisting of cooked shrimp in a mixture of whole or diced tomatoes, the "holy trinity" of onion, celery and bell pepper, spiced with hot pepper sauce or cayenne-based seasoning, and served over steamed or boiled white rice.

- Origin (Wikidata): United States

### Cabbage roll

A cabbage roll is a dish consisting of cooked cabbage leaves wrapped around a variety of fillings.

- From: Midwestern US, United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): rice, meat, vegetable, onion, Brassica oleracea var. capitata, edible mushroom, minced meat

Fun facts:

- (about) Cooking textbook author Nancy Krcek stated that the origins of the dish are unclear and that it is possible multiple groups of people invented it at the same time.
- (culture) Meat fillings are traditional in Europe, and include beef, lamb, or pork seasoned with garlic, onion, and spices.
- (about) Another cooking book author, Malgorzata Caprari, stated it is believed that credit is owed to the poorer inhabitants of Central and Eastern European countries.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) Due to the widespread cultivation of cabbage in these regions, it is likely the cultures who inhabited them were the original inventors of this dish.
- (culture) Traditionally made with pork, beef, bacon, rice, spices and aromatics, the cabbage rolls are broiled in a tomato sauce and served with polenta, sour cream and spicy pickled peppers.

### Cincinnati chili

Cincinnati chili (or Cincinnati-style chili) is a Mediterranean-spiced meat sauce used as a topping for spaghetti or hot dogs ("coneys").

- From: Midwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): ground beef

Fun facts:

- (history) Both dishes were developed by immigrant restaurateurs in the Cincinnati area during the 1920s.
- (inspiration) The dish does not contain chocolate, despite popular myth to the contrary.
- (about) Customary toppings include cheddar cheese, onions, and beans; specific combinations of toppings are known as "ways".

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) The most popular order is a "three-way", which adds shredded cheese to the chili-topped spaghetti (a "two-way"), while a "four-way" adds onions or beans before topping with the cheese.
- (about) In 2000, one local chili parlor was named an America's Classic by the James Beard Foundation, and in 2013, Smithsonian named the same chili parlor one of the "20 Most Iconic Food Destinations in America".
- (origin) Cincinnati chili comes from United States.

### Fried chicken

Fried chicken, more formally known as Southern fried chicken, is a dish consisting of chicken pieces, coated with seasoned flour or batter, that are pan-fried, deep-fried, pressure-fried, or air-fried.

- From: Midwestern US, United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): chicken as food

Fun facts:

- (history) However, the Scottish were the first to have been recorded as deep-frying their chicken in fat with breadcrumbs and seasonings, as evidenced by a recipe in a 1747 cookbook by Hannah Glasse and a 1773 diary entry describing fried chicken on the Isle of Skye.
- (about) The first dish known to have been deep-fried was fritters, which were popular in the European Middle Ages.
- (about) The first known recipe in the US did not contain the seasonings that were in the earlier Scottish recipe.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) There is an English cookbook from 1736 which mentions fried chicken, the "Dictionarium Domesticum", by Nathan Bailey, where it is called "a marinade of chickens".

Goulash is a meal made of meat and vegetables seasoned with paprika and other spices.

- Origin (Wikidata): Kingdom of Hungary
- Ingredients (Wikidata): carrot, meat, butter, flour, spice, olive oil, chili pepper, soy sauce, Agaricus, tomato paste

Fun facts:

- (origin) Originating in Hungary, goulash is a common meal predominantly eaten in Central Europe but also in other parts of Europe.
- (culture) Goulash is one of the national dishes of Hungary and a symbol of the country.
- (history) Its origins may be traced back as far as the 10th century to stews eaten by Hungarian shepherds.

### Pancake

A pancake, also known as a hotcake, griddlecake, or flapjack, is a flat type of batter bread like cake, often thin and round, prepared from a starch-based batter that may contain eggs, milk, and butter, and then cooked on a hot surface such as a griddle or frying pan.

- From: Midwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): milk, flour, oil, egg as food

Fun facts:

- (origin) A variation originating from southeast Europe is palatschinke, a thin moist pancake fried on both sides and filled with jam, cream cheese, chocolate, or ground walnuts, but many other fillings—sweet or savoury—can also be used.
- (about) When buttermilk is used in place of or in addition to milk, the pancake develops a tart flavor and becomes known as a buttermilk pancake, which is common in Scotland, Ireland and the US.
- (about) In Britain and the Commonwealth, they are associated with Shrove Tuesday, commonly known as "Pancake Day", when, historically, perishable ingredients had to be used up before the fasting period of Lent.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (origin) Pancake comes from United States.

### Italian ice

- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (about) In Philadelphia and its metropolitan area Italian ice is known as water ice.

- (culture) Traditionally lemon-flavored, popular modern choices include cherry, strawberry, and other fruits and confections.

- (culture) Traditionally it was not served as a dessert pie, but instead as a breakfast food with hot coffee.

### Frybread

Frybread (also spelled fry bread) is a dish of the indigenous people of North America that is a flat dough bread, fried or deep-fried in oil, shortening, or lard.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States

Fun facts:

- (culture) The ingredients for frybread were provided to Native Americans to prevent them from starving when they were moved from areas where they could grow and forage their traditional foods to areas that would not support their traditional foods.
- (origin) Frybread comes from United States.
- (culture) Critics see the dish as both a symbol of colonization and a symbol of resilience.

### Tamale pie

Tamale pie is a pie and casserole dish associated with the cuisine of the Southwestern United States.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Named after: tamale

Fun facts:

- (culture) The first tamale pies were made at the turn of the 20th century, at a time when tamales were a popular street food in America.
- (history) From the 1920s through 1950s, tamale pies were a staple of the American diet, promoted by the government in times of Depression and war as a way to stretch meat supplies, and enjoyed in all times for its perceived accessible exoticism and ability to keep as it stood at potlucks and Sunday suppers.
- (inspiration) Tamale pie is named after tamale.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

### Orange (fruit)

The orange, also called sweet orange to distinguish it from the bitter orange (Citrus × aurantium), is the fruit of a tree in the family Rutaceae.

- From: Hawaii, United States
- Named after: Portugal, Malta, China
- Ingredients (Wikidata): pulp, seed, locule, orange peel

Fun facts:

- (history) They first appear in Western art in the Arnolfini Portrait by Jan van Eyck, but they had been depicted in Chinese art centuries earlier, as in Zhao Lingrang's Song dynasty fan painting Yellow Oranges and Green Tangerines.
- (origin) The orange originated in a region encompassing Southern China, Northeast India, and Myanmar; the earliest mention of the sweet orange was in Chinese literature in 314 BC.
- (inspiration) Orange is named after Portugal, Malta, China.

### Salt pork

Salt pork is salt-cured pork.

- From: Louisiana, United States

Fun facts:

- (history) Along with hardtack and corned beef, salt pork was a standard ration for many militaries and navies throughout the 17th, 18th, and 19th centuries, seeing usage in the American Civil War, War of 1812, and the Napoleonic Wars, among others.
- (culture) Salt pork now finds use in traditional American cuisine, particularly Boston baked beans, pork and beans, and to add its flavor to vegetables cooked in water, as with greens in soul food.
- (culture) Salt pork that contains a significant amount of meat, resembling standard side bacon, is known as "streak o' lean." It is traditionally popular in the Southeastern United States.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) It may be eaten as one would eat bacon or used to season other dishes like traditional salt pork.

### Wheat

- From: Louisiana, United States

- (history) Since 1960, world production of wheat and other grain crops has tripled and is expected to grow further through the middle of the 21st century.
- (about) Wheat is grown on a larger area of land than any other food crop (219.5 million hectares or 542 million acres in 2024).

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) In 2024, world wheat production was 799 million tonnes (881 million short tons), making it the second most-produced cereal after maize (known as corn in North America and Australia).

### Ranch dressing

### Bizcochito

The bizcochito or biscochito is a New Mexican crisp butter cookie made with lard, flavored with sugar, cinnamon, and anise.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): sugar, cinnamon, butter, lard, anise oil

Fun facts:

- (history) The roots of this pastry date back as far as the Battle of Puebla in 1862, where French Emperor Maximilian was overthrown by the Mexicans.
- (about) The cookie was developed in New Mexico over the centuries from the first Spanish colonists of what was then known as Santa Fe de Nuevo México.
- (culture) The dough is rolled thin and cut into the shape of the fleur-de-lis, the Christian cross, a star, or a circle, symbolizing the moon.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) This date is now famously recognized in the United States as Cinco de Mayo, literally the "Fifth of May".
- (culture) Biscochitos are commonly served during celebrations such as wedding receptions, baptisms, and religious (especially Catholic) holidays, and frequently during the Christmas season.
